About 80% and 90% of us have also a third muscle known as the plantaris, but since the plantaris is believed to be largely vestigial from a functional standpoint, it is often lumped in with the gastrocnemius. The gastroc clearly steals a show as it creates the coveted diamond sweep in the lower leg that is the envy of millions. I will develop gastrocnemius test to the world. Hey, I've been blessed by the calf muscle gods with good genetics and probably at least a little bit fit too. This is a two joint muscle that spans both the knee and the ankle joint with its medial and lateral heads originating at the posterior aspects of the distal femur. It then coalesces with the soleus inferiorly to insert at the calcaneus or the heel bone via the achilles tendon. Due to its high density of fast twitch fibers, we depend on the gastric for rapid powerful contractions for explosive ankle plantar flexion, such as when we sprint or jump. Since it originates above the knee, it works with the hamstrings to flex and stabilize the knee joint as well. Next, we have the soleus. Deep to the gastroc is the less glamorous soleus muscle. And although you rarely hear about someone wanting to get a jacked soleus, it does play an important role in our ankle function. Unlike the gastroc, the soleus muscle fibers are largely slow twitch between 70 and 96%, making it more of a postural muscle that is resistant to fatigue. This design is perfect for activities that require more plantar flexion endurance, such as the cases of walking or jogging. As you can see, even though the gastroc and the soleus muscle lie very intimately with each other, they are vastly different in their qualities and characteristic. It's almost like the gastroc is the flashy superhero who gets all of the credit and the soleus is his trust ever dependable, but less celebrated sidekick. Now let's go full nerd and discuss the functions of these muscles by reviewing the biomechanics of the motion we will be hammering today, which is ankle plantar flexion. Plantar flexion is the action of raising your heels off the ground or pressing a gas pedal. When we do a standing heel race, we create a rare mechanical occurrence in the human body, a second class lever. As with all second class levers, ankle plantar flexion occurs with a load in the middle of the system. In case of a heel race, the load is our body weight. The effort or force factor shown here at the insertion of the cap muscle complex is on the other side of the load shown here at the first metacarpophyland g.l. joint, aka the ball of your foot. Imagine you're picking up a wheelbarrow full of bricks. That same mechanism is what's happening within your ankle and foot as you lift your heels off the ground. Don't worry, the physics lingo is almost over, so please stay with me. If you have ever felt the burn of a calf race in your foot muscles rather than in your calf muscles, this could be a sign that you aren't properly utilizing your second class lever. If you find this to be a barrier as you try out today's exercise, drop the weight a little bit or try some toe extension stretches. All right, let's train the calves. Set up. Since we want to maximize mechanical tension throughout the entire plantar flexion range of motion available to us, the exercise says we're going to do today. Think back to what we learned about the calf muscles in my first video first. Well, the gastroc thrives in an explosive fast switch environment. The soleus usually enjoys lower twitch endurance settings. Since everyone has their own individual strength and limitations, I'd recommend that you start with a range of eight to 12 reps and listen to what the muscle is telling you to determine when to increase the reps and when to add resistance. When we reach the point of technical failure and can no longer isolate the calf muscles without compensation at the foot or knee, you found your sweet spot. Now let's start off sets. As in most cases of muscle hypertrophy, what we want to do is we want to maximize our accumulation of volume. Usually that means anywhere from three to six sets of an exercise per training session, depending on your training history. So start with fear and gradually increase your sets over several weeks. If you wake up the day after training calves and can't put your foot flat on the ground with our intense soreness, you've probably done too much. So scale it back a little so you can perform a calf workout two to four times per week. Keep in mind that this is not a metabolic conditioning workout. We don't want to rush or rest intervals at the expense of not being able to achieve our volume goals in the subsequent sets. 60 to 90 seconds of rest is pretty much a good place to start to allow your calves to recover before starting the next set without leaving reps on the table at the end of your workout. Now let's get deep into the exercises. Now it's important that we don't neglect the soleus muscle group. A lot of people tend to perform the bulk of their calf training standing up, but if we really, uh, mix both sitting and standing exercises, we can really maximize the shape of our calves. Since the soleus only crosses the ankle joint, it is a single joint muscle. This has led to the tradition of the soleus exercises being performed with any bends in order to decrease tension on the gas drug and more effectively bias the soleus, a concept known as active insufficiency. I do want to point out that research has shown that the soleus and gas drug don't simply turn on and off because the knees bent or straight. So even if you are doing calf exercises with the knee extended, the soleus is still helping out a little bit. For the seated heel raise, find a piece of selectorized equipment dedicated to this movement, but you can also set yourself up with a couple of dumbbells. Just place your forefeet on a block or plate and rest the dumbbells on your knees. Press the balls of your feet to raise the heels off the ground. Hold at the top, then slowly return to the starting position. If you're looking for something a little bit spicier that involves more than just the calf muscles, do the same exercise in a squat position. You can use a rig to help you balance, squat down to parallel and hold an isometric squat as you raise the heels off the ground. Soleus exercise number two is a single leg strength drill. When you are standing on one leg, the muscle that crosses the ankles have to contract to keep the joint stable. And the soleus is no different. When the knee travels forward over the toes, the soleus eccentrically contracts to ensure that the knee translates anteriorly with control. As you come back up to standing, the soleus concentrically contracts to bring the tibia back to vertical. So when you are doing a compound single leg strength movement, like the skater squat, pistol squat, Bulgarian split squats and single leg deadlifts, your soleus is hard at work as it helps you maintain your balance. Soleus exercise number three, walk it out. Unlike its counterpart, the soleus muscle is a partial muscle with a higher proportion of type one fibers, meaning that is great for lower intensity, longer duration activities that require muscle endurance. Gastroc exercise number four, jumping and sprinting. === FILE: Do You Need To Lift Heavy Weights To Get Big Muscles？ ｜ Quick Tips [nqbUSRXWEJc].txt ===

 what's going on everyone? Today we're gonna clear up some misconceptions regarding strength and hypertrophy. There seems to be a lot of misunderstanding regarding whether you need to lift really heavy weights in order to get bigger muscles, or if you can lift light weights and still get big muscles. The truth is, the answer lies somewhere in between, and that's what we're gonna be exploring today. So which comes first? Strength or hypertrophy? It's like the question of the chicken or the egg. This question has plagued bro scientists for ages. A lot of people try to improve their way they look by gaining muscle and losing fat, but not everyone appreciates the role that strength plays in that equation. So, what exactly? Let's talk about that. Hypertrophy is muscle growth in response to working against forces that we're not used to. In order to stimulate this, we need to be strong enough to challenge the working muscle. Specifically, those large fibers that are only recruited when high forces are required. We find those high forces with relatively heavy weights, or when fatigue has exhausted all of the other smaller fibers. So where does strength come in? We need to build strength in order to reach those large fibers that grow in response to training. That's why beginners can see rapid strength development without much hypertrophy in the first few weeks of training. As a muscle adapts, force production improves, you need to keep using heavier and heavier weights over time. You can't totally separate strength from hypertrophy since we need more force over time in order to continue stimulating muscle growth. So in turn, hypertrophy augments strength since a bigger muscle has the potential to become a stronger muscle. The take-home message is that if you're training to lose fat and build muscle but you don't emphasize strength development, you're missing an important piece of the body composition puzzle. I hope this clears it up and you got something from it. As always, if you have any questions, drop them down below and see you guys next time. Bye!

breaking the floor the first one's gonna be a deficit deadlift the deficit deadlift we're looking for anywhere from one to two inches of elevation once you have your pad or block that you're going to use we're gonna take the bar and perform a deadlift as we normally would or we're really gonna focus on feeling the engagement of the hamstrings the reason why we use this block is because creating a deficit makes it a little bit easier for you to put the weight in the hamstrings a posterior chain instead of trying to drive off the floor with your quads it also increases the difficulty of the movement by increasing your range of motion by one to two inches depending on how big your block is the second movement we're gonna recommend is the stiff leg deadlift this is a similar idea to the deficit deadlift except instead of using a block you're just putting yourself in a compromised position by stretching your hamstring more and not allowing yourself to lower your hips to a point that's most optimal just like the last exercise we're gonna focus on all of the things we talked about earlier so building tension in the hamstrings before pulling off the floor wedging yourself under the bar like a teeter-totter and we're gonna use the same grip and foot stance that you would for your regular conventional deadlift stiff leg deadlift and Romanian deadlift often get confused so I think it's important to make a distinction between the two stiff leg deadlift starts from the floor like a regular deadlift whereas a Romanian deadlift you're gonna pick the bar up like normal and you're === FILE: How Injury Prevention Might Not Be Working Like You Think ｜ Scientific [dGixC-FyQhs].en.txt ===

I see this word being tossed around endlessly on social media specific and no really complex exercises are being sold with an injury prevention tab with no real evidence or logic behind them some people claim that fixing alignment for postures and making movements fit their criteria of perfect prevents injuries but they ignore the fact that defining good versus bad movement is nearly impossible and there is no way to establish a direct causal relationship between habitual movement patterns and a particular injury the truth is injuries are multivariate problems and there's no specific exercise we can do to completely prevent them prevention is not a lot attempting to maintain every single joint in some kind of ideal position or about being able to perform complex corrective exercises with best humans are not perfectly symmetrical and there is in fact no research to support the idea that correcting imbalances leads to reduction in future injuries for a specific movement or screen to qualify as an injury prevention tool it would have to satisfy three main criteria first the movement needs to demonstrate a strong association with injury risk second the significant Association must reliably identify at high risk and at low risk subgroups since low injury risk individuals often appear to have abnormal movement patterns third a specific intervention to repair the deficit must reduce injury risk and a high-risk subgroup to an effect greater than the same intervention given to the entire sample population there's nothing in the market that comes close to meeting this criteria we know training related injuries occur when the load applied to the tissues exceeds the load bearing capacity or seller simply put it it's not the load that breaks you down it's the load you're unprepared for the first part of this two-sided equation is a result of smart training strategies that take into account load management first and foremost minimize exposure to loads or unprepared for a logical progression from day-to-day and week-to-week allows your body to adapt to impose stimuli and train without within the limits of capacity and recovery the second part of the equation has to do with building up capacity or tolerance this happens both via compound movements like your squad bench and deadlift and also via accessory exercise that's the stuff that will prepare you for both the expected and unexpected prevention is not the product of endless functional exercises you must prepare your body for increasing workloads and intensity over a long period of time with effective and intelligent programming if prevention is successful it's not necessarily because of the movement correction but it could just be an adaptation that it's not specific to that particular exercise if you have obvious muscular imbalances or range of motion discrepancies that leave you unable to perform simple movements without falling on your face or grunting with its comfort then you should be working on that through that process you should understand that the most important principle of injury prevention is our the concept of physical preparation and loudness

=== FILE: How To GROW Your Hamstrings The Right Way ｜ Quick Tips With Stefi Cohen [xx3A5z9ULyk].txt ===

 If you're a trainer, you've probably said it, and if you're an athlete, you've probably heard it. Keep the weight close to your body. That is a very common cue used for many exercises, including Olympic lifts and deadlifts, both sumo and conventional. This probably is why I get an uproar of questions and concerns anytime I post a video of me doing an RDL or any other movement that allows the weight to travel farther away from my body. Now, listen up, there's a method to the madness. Keep in mind that training for pure strength and training to isolate a specific muscle group are two completely different things. When we're trying to move a weight from point A to point B with the highest level of mechanical efficiency, of course we want to keep the bar or the weight close, so close that you might turn loose some skin over the matter. We want to use mechanical advantages to leverage our weight against that of the barbell, and a slight deviation from a vertical bar-bath could sabotage the entire lift. On the other hand, if you want to intentionally target a muscle and put as much tension on it as possible, we should actually try to put it at a mechanical disadvantage by moving the dumbbells slightly away from our center of mass, which is going to increase the distance between the weight and the joint, in this case causing the hamstrings and glutes to contract harder to control the hip-hingeing movement. In earlier terms, I'm increasing the lever arm by moving the resistance further away from the axis of rotation, in this case the hip, leading to increased torque in the hip-exensor muscles. I usually aim to keep the dumbbells hovering just over my forefoot, like this, because this equals more torque and stress in the muscles, which in the long run will lead to more growth and more strength. Something else I want to address with the RDL is changing your ankle position. We'll often see lifters and body builders and power lifters including myself, elevating their toes in order to increase dorsiflexion. This adjustment facilitates increased hip dominance of the already hip-focused exercise by putting the calf muscle on a stretch, which further restricts knee flexion. Hopefully you regal the hamstrings, our two joint muscle groups, so we can impart more tension onto them and further put them at a disadvantage with simultaneous knee extension and hip flexion without having to lock out the knees completely. Now what you want to feel with this movement is a deep but tolerable hamstring stretch at the bottom of the exercise. So when it comes to getting bigger and stronger, muscle tension is good, but neural tension is not so good, so perform the toes elevated RDL without comfortably uncomfortable range and over time you'll not only see strength inside size gains in your hand pops, but you may also be able to access new ranges of motion, especially if you're a ten-man who can't touch his toes or her toes with ease. If you want to blow up your ham is, ham is in a good way, try this style of RDL and let me know how it goes. Please answer the three next questions on the screen. Please share this video, comment on this video so we can continue making these high quality content for you guys. As always, thank you so much for your support, it means the world to me and I'll catch you guys next time. Peace.

=== FILE: Is Stretching Effective For Injury Prevention？ ｜ Stefi Vs Stretching [2NRe5gseWWI].en.txt ===

lots of weekend warriors and bro athletes tend to think that injury prevention is all about having a good stretching regime no you shouldn't searching doesn't really work the way people think and it definitely doesn't prevent injuries or improve performance in fact most flexible athletes are not always the best or strongest athletes studies actually confirmed that pre exercise stretching has no significant effect on performance and that it actually might have the opposite effect it might have been reduced muscle strength and increase injury susceptibility especially in the absence of increased control in those newly found ranges people who feel stiff and tight assume their range of motion is limited by literally shortened muscles but this is rarely the case despite how it feels to you being stiff is not the same as being inflexible in fact they're not even related stiffness is a sensation rather than an actual limitation the nervous system is the master conductor determining how far a person can stretch so when you stretch that doesn't necessarily lead to a structural change in your body's muscles or tendons but rather it creates an increased stretched tolerance caused by adaptations in our nervous system well lots of things feel good without having a clear physiological benefit Hayden ice cream chiropractic adjustments massage are just a few examples of that I understand that we might expect something that feels good to work well too but science has shown over and over again that's stretching chiropractic adjustments and massage are not exactly saving the world

=== FILE: Should you use a belt when you’re lifting？ Advice from a 25x world record holding powerlifter [G56y0kI4l7o].en.txt ===

you don't need a belt but if you want to learn how to lift the most amount of weight possible you'd have to learn how to use it belts don't protect you against back injuries they're not going to lift the weight for you but when you learn how to use them properly they function as an external tactile cue that allows you to improve your ability to brace during a lift to use the belt properly you want to wrap it around your midsection and you want it tight but not too tight so you want to be able to still put one finger in between the belt and your belly next you want to take a deep breath in and expand your belly against the belt but not too much because so you might pass out my recommendation is to start using a belt at weights of 80 percent or more and don't depend on it

=== FILE: Straight To The Point Movements For Huge Triceps ｜ Tips From Stefi Cohen [RlCEOyLEpJo].txt ===

 let's be real, everybody wants bigger arms, so listen up. Whether you want to have a stronger bench press or you simply want to own a pair of horseshoes that would make sea biscuit jealous, this video is for you. So here are my top three exercises to build massive triceps. Number one, skull crusher. By getting the shoulder into a more flexed position, we will be working the tricep group in their most lengthened position. This allows us to impart more mechanical tension specifically through the long head of the triceps, which is the only tricep head that crosses the shoulder joint as well as the elbow joint. You want to make sure that you're moving with control and intent as you allow the weight to stretch your shoulder into a slightly more flexion at the bottom of the movement. So try this movement with an easy bar, but do yourself a favor and don't actually crush your skull. Get it? Number two, we have the cable pushdown. This is a classic movement and is a staple in tricep training and for good reason. While the skull crusher works the tricep group in a more lengthened range, the cable pushdown focuses on the muscle contraction through mid-range. The key here is slightly hinging at the hips so that the rope or straight bar is slightly in front of you. If you're using a rope, you can even get a little bit of abduction into that final squeeze. Finally, we have the cable tricep kickback. The kickback is no longer just for step aerobics. By putting the shoulder in a hyper-extended position, we put the tricep group in a shortened position and can generate a pretty intense contraction at their end range. I absolutely love doing these on one arm at a time to really focus on that lockout. As with many other isolation exercises, cables are often superior to dumbbells to maintain constant tension on the muscle you're trying to train. So there you have it, the ingredients for the best tricep pump that you will ever have. As always, thank you guys for watching. Thanks for your support. Make sure you tag and share this video as many times as you can. And as always, I'll catch you guys on the next video. Peace. You're like, I'm in vain. Stephanie going here. Welcome back. Today, I'm going to be taking you through my workout and I want to show you a little bit about what I've been up to and the type of training that I'm doing out of now.

to Newton's third law whenever we move we impart a force on an external object that can overcome an equivalent internally directed force both sprinting and squatting can be reduced down to forces that act on objects but when we talk about how to develop strength we really need to be specific in basic physics force is mass times acceleration to generate force we can either move heavy weights or we muscle fiber is a bundle of contractile structures called myofibrils that work together to create a muscle contraction that pulls the two attachment points closer together it resists elongation or maintains a fixed position against an external load these are called concentric eccentric and isometric construct contractions respectively now each myofibril is made up of a series of structures called sarcomeres each sarcomere contains an overlapping protein structure where thin acting filaments are juxtaposed with thick myosin filaments that form the basis of any muscle contraction when we move an electrical signal from the motor nerve is translated into a chemical signal that slides the overlapping proteins against each other by creating and breaking temporary bonds between the actin and myosin filaments this process generates the force that reduces the distance between the ends of the muscle against a load the key takeaway here is that muscles shorten because of chemical reactions in the muscle that are then turned into mechanical forces this lower the potential movement the more time a muscle has to form and break more actin myosin bonds or cross bridges in order to produce force and the faster the movement the less a muscle has to create the same bonds this means that force potential in fast contractions is lower even at maximal efforts this force velocity relationship forms a curve which is important to understand if you want to develop strength light weights can be moved very fast but because of the way muscle contraction works the amount of potential force a muscle can produce in a fast contraction is relatively low heavyweights cannot be moved nearly as fast obviously so their force potential is much higher and there is an inverse relationship between muscle force and muscle velocity as force goes up velocity goes down and vice versa that is the force velocity curve when we lift heavier weights we learn to produce more force when we lift lighter weights or sprint we'll learn to produce more speed if the goal is to squat more weight we need to spend more time training with heavier weights than with lighter weights this is one of several key components of the set principle or specific adaptations to imposed demands next let's talk about strength adaptations many different changes follow different kinds of strength training it's easy to get lost in granular concepts like the venetian angle fiber type shifts and metabolic changes among others so we will focus on four mechanisms that are specific to the heavy lifting that === FILE: To Track or Not To Track Your Macros？ ｜ Stefi Cohen Explains [fdxBh6XJ5ro].txt ===

 ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus ascidentus is not always 100% accurate for several reasons. While caloric intake is largely dictated by the foods that we eat, it can also be affected by what types of food we are eating. Thus, energy intake may not always be equal to energy utilization. For example, the body tends to absorb more energy from cooked and processed foods compared to raw or more minimally processed foods. Yet another reason to strive for a wide variety of wholesome minimally processed foods as much as possible, especially if weight loss is the goal. It is also important to realize that food labels are often inaccurate by as much as 25% in terms of calories and micronutrient amounts. In restaurants, underestimate calories of meals by as much as 18%. Our human imperfections can also complicate things as it has been shown that adults miss remember and miscount food amounts by as much as 1000 calories per day. That can really add up and cause some frustration in the long run. So what is one to do? Weigh every morsel of food to the gram and live life tracking calories to the T, not at all. Remember, the trick is to find the nutrition lifestyle that works best for you in the long run. If having a food scale with you wherever you go isn't going to be realistic, let's be honest, that definitely would not be realistic for 99% of us. There are other methods of tracking that allow for increased flexibility. Keep in mind that none of these methods are going to be entirely effortless. Some may be simpler than others, but their effectiveness relies on your ability to use them correctly and accurately. There may be some friction during the initial stages of building these habits as you increase your awareness of portion sizes and nutrient content of different types of food, but making choices that will be more conducive to your goals will become relatively second nature with practice and time. Let's talk about the no tracking necessary approach that I'm talking about. If you find that more regimented tracking is not for you, you may choose the non-tracking route. While you won't be aiming for very specific numbers to hit in terms of calories or macros, this approach does come with some fundamental guidelines. You want to make sure that you're including a serving of lean protein with every meal. You want to have one to two servings of fruits and veggies with every meal, avoid snacking, and instead focus on eating complete meals. Focus on eating wholesome, minimally processed foods most of the time, about 80% of the time. Emphasize having a bit more carbohydrates around your workouts, so pre and post. Try to eat three to six meals per day based on your schedule and preferences while following the guidelines above. Keep eating out to a minimum. If you plan on going out to eat, then adjust the rest of your day of eating accordingly. Fewer calories, higher satiety index. While you will not be required to weigh and record any food with the non-tracking approach, using the hand portion guide below will help you gauge your portion sizes more consistently and honestly. Let's face it, we all wish a serving size of peanut butter was a little bit bigger. In terms of tracking protein and calories, if you are comfortable with tracking a few numbers throughout the day, you may try the protein and calories method. This approach is slightly more hands-on than the non-tracking method described above, requiring daily tracking of protein intake and overall calories consumed during the day. As long as the protein target is attained, the athlete can choose which ratio of carbs and fats he or she would like to eat on that day, allowing for that increased flexibility. This method speaks to the fact that when adequate protein intake is attained, the remaining calories can be a variety of ratios of fat to carbs, and no one ratio has been deemed superior in terms of weight loss. The athlete should continue to strive for consistency with the seven principles listed above. In terms of tracking macronutrients, if you are willing to weigh and record the majority of your food, tracking macronutrients may be a feasible option for you. With this tracking method, you have three very specific quantitative targets to aim for and meet daily for each macronutrient. We're talking about your carbohydrates, proteins, and fats. Macronutrient targets are usually represented in terms of caloric percentages, which will provide some mathematical efforts on the athlete's part. For example, with a balanced macronutrient split, an athlete would typically aim for 40% of his or her total calories to come from carbs, 30% of calories to come from protein, and 30% of calories to come from fat each day. So if the calorie goal of that day is 2000 calories, you must multiply your total calories by each percentage, then divide by the particular macronutrient caloric density. So depending on the athlete's preferences, the macronutrient split can be adjusted. For instance, a lower carb approach may look more like 30% carbs, 40% protein, and 30% fat. In terms of macro ranges, hitting a specific macronutrient target each day is overwhelming to some and tedious to others. Some people who have moved away from rigid macro tracking have reported nearly force feeding themselves at night in order to hit the macros. On the other hand, some athletes found that they were depriving themselves of food even when they were genuinely hungry, just because it would put them over their macros. For this reasons, our hybrid nutrition coaches will often recommend a range of macronutrients to hit rather than a specific number. While the magnitude of these ranges can vary based on the goals and experience level of the client, recommendations could range from 500 to 700 calories, 10 to 20 grams of each macro daily. The client then is allowed to be a bit more intuitive and flexible, eating more or less based on his or her hunger throughout the day. Reaching a specific minimum amount of protein is typically recommended as is sticking to the seven nutrition principles listed above. The five days on, two days off method. This strategy is a delicate balance of flexibility and structure, where the client eats in a relatively large deficit during the week, hence five days on and two off. And they eat closer to their maintenance calories on the weekends. This is often a feasible option for clients who have a traditional nine to five work schedule during the week, but like to go out or drink or spend some time with family and friends during the weekends without having to closely monitor or track their intake. Although the name may suggest that the weekends are somewhat of a free for all strategy, this strategy still requires that the client consistently follows the seven principles listed at the beginning of the segment. It is possible to nullify the caloric deficit that was achieved during the week in just a few indulgent meals. So maintaining awareness of intake and healthy moderation is still paramount. If you are interested in a more involved, hands-on tracking approach, you will need some tools to get started. First, make sure you have some tools at hand, such as a food scale, measuring cup and spoons. These items will help increase your accuracy with tracking food amounts and give you specific quantities to enter into your tracking app. While some people prefer food journaling with pen and paper, modern technology has made tracking way easier than ever before. Several of these apps have huge databases from countless foods from different brands, including many restaurant items, which further increases your accuracy with tracking. Many also use scanning features that allow you to use your camera to scan foods barcodes, which automatically searches for you, the specific item, saving you time and effort in your tracking endeavors. In terms of apps, my fitness pal is the most commonly used tracking app. But there are some others like Lifesum, Fitbit, and MyPlate that offer similar services. Download a few of them, try them, and see which one you like best. Keep in mind that the accuracy of your app is only as good as the accuracy of your entries, which is why your measurements are important. Logging a deep fried chicken drumstick as a raw, boneless, skinless chicken thigh is going to create some significant discrepancies. So when in doubt, try to log your food as accurately as possible. I do wanna point out that tracking should never become an obsession. If you feel like your relationship with tracking food is becoming unhealthy or that you may be showing signs of developing an eating disorder, you should definitely consult your physician. === FILE: Training Intensity & Volume Explained By Stefi Cohen [sO6Zdz6A5iA].txt ===

 Today I'm going to talk about how to balance training intensity and volume. Periodization or the organization of your workouts in powerlifting training is often underappreciated or worse, completely butchered. On one hand, we can find lifters running the same mononus-sema-repsin sets for months on end with no credence to the principles of progressive overload, wondering why they aren't making any progress. On the other hand, we have lifters who redline every single workout, making out at an RP20 for each and every set that they do, also wondering why they're hitting a plateau in their performance. Finding a harmonious balance between training intensity, how heavier lifting, and volume, how much work we're doing, quantified by total reps, sets, and loads is the key to making continuous progress while minimizing risk of overtraining and injury. This balance is also crucial to ensure that the lifter is as prepared as possible for competition day. The ideal combination of volume and intensity will shift over the course of a training macrocycle to mitigate fatigue as much as possible. Progress and fatigue tend to be inversely related. So if our goal is to optimize performance and progress, excessive fatigue is our enemy. This is why the athlete who frequently exceeds his or her own capacity for training volume and intensity won't succeed in the long run. So more is not always better. Believe me, because I had to learn that. That's not bad. It feels great to push past barriers and training and this can be done strategically, but what matters most is that you execute just enough volume to drive the desired adaptations without impeding upon your performance in future sessions. This target is known as your maximum or maximum recoverable volume or MRV. At the other end of the spectrum, there's athletes who are well below this capacity and unable to provide a strong enough stimulus to stimulate muscle growth and strength. This athlete may need a boost in volume to achieve the appropriate loading dosage. This concept is known as your minimum effective volume or MEV, the threshold of work that must be reached in training to make steady measurable improvements. Athletes have shown that anywhere from 5 to 20 sets per week can be enough to drive strength and hypertrophy gains, but that's a pretty large range. Moreover, high volume is relative and individual to each athlete. A high amount of volume for one person may be well below another athlete's MEV. So instead of defining yourself an arbitrary number of sets to break through a training plateau, try boosting your current volume by 20% for 8 to 12 weeks and then reassessing from there. Now, back to how volume and intensity will ideally intermingle with a periodized training program. During the accumulation phase, volume is high and intensity is moderate. This 4 to 6 week phase is when the lifter sees the greatest gains in work capacity, level acquisition and hypertrophy. Next comes the intensification phase, which is when, you guessed it, intensity increases. Well, the number of sets slightly decreases during this 3 to 6 weeks. Overall work done is set to increase during this phase. Fatigue is high, but things really start to come together in terms of noticeable progress. As the competition approaches, volume will be tapered back even more during the 1 to 2 weeks of the peaking phase. This is to prevent unwanted fatigue accumulation, which could be detrimental to an athlete's performance during midday. During this time, intensity stays high to provide adequate stimulus for maximal force production. But performing fewer sets helps mitigate the risk of excessive tissue damage and subsequent inflammation that can be associated with high volume training. If you follow the program and everything goes as anticipated, which I might add it rarely ever does, so be ready for hiccups, you will be perfectly peaked for your testing day and as prepared as possible to take that barbell for a ride. Volume and intensity play a cute game of cat and mouse during the training program, and that's just to be expected. So I hope this video helped clarify at least the rationale behind training periodization. As always, thank you guys for watching, thank you guys for the support. If you have any questions, drop them down below or reach out to me on any of my social media channels and I'll try to get back to you. As always, thank you guys and I'll see you guys on the next video.

=== FILE: Why do people arch their back when they bench？ And is it dangerous？  #fitness #fitnessmotivation [cBqppt5Us0Q].en.txt ===

why the do powerlifters bench like this and is it cheating you probably think about this when you see people benching like that and you might think that arching your back like this should be reserved for contortionist only but actually this is how all the pros do it I'm a 25 time world record holding powerlifter and this advice will literally save your shoulders and help you lift bigger weight arching your upper back puts the shoulder joints in a much more stable position by increasing the congruency or the fit of the shoulder blade and your armb bone and it increases the tension on the lats that wrap around your torso making it a much safer position to bench in finally by bringing your chest up you're simulating a decline bench which favors the PEC major fibers that are a much bigger portion of the PEC these three things give your shoulders much more stability more strength and thus a bigger bench so try it for yourself

=== FILE: here are some solid cues that will make your squat stronger @gymshark  #paidpartnership [Lc_eryvPXfk].en.txt ===

here's how you fix your Squat and add another 10 lbs to your list in 30 days yes that's me squatting 500 lb and I had to break a ton of platea to get there start by creating a super tight shelf on the Barlo on your back and hold it as close to you as possible scw your feet out and turn your legs out as well think about putting your hips in between your feet as you descend into the bottom of the squat you should be moving fast yet under control by activ bending at the hips bringing your attention to the muscles in the front of your hip and tensing and Contracting them the more stable and solid your hips are the stronger your lift is going to be so try it for yourself and let me know how it goes
